## AI Research

### [Minimum required pacing: finish aligning it before you train the next one](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZWSstGSwmaxTjQ8W7/minimum-required-pacing-finish-aligning-it-before-you-train)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's say we coordinate to pace the frontier, but we don't coordinate on an outright pause. What should the standard of pacing be?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My proposal for a minimum requirement: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a lab cannot train the next generation of models (in terms of capabilities) until they have adequately</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6n42svad2it"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6n42svad2it">[1]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> resolved all significant alignment issues in the present generation of models.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don't launch a jet that goes at Mach 3 before you've figured out </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">why</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your Mach 2 jet keeps crashing</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref40kow7g086n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn40kow7g086n">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. With any other kind of dangerous technology, we wouldn't accept "shrug and move on".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This proposal may appeal to many prosaic-alignment optimists </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> pessimists, because they usually differ on how easy it would be to fix </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">current</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> alignment issues with current techniques. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prosaic-alignment optimists might expect that a moderate coordinated slowdown would suffice to adequately resolve alignment issues at each generation.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prosaic-alignment pessimists might expect it to result in an extended pause, buying time to discover new fundamental approaches to aligned AI.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it creates much better incentives for labs: in order to advance in capabilities, they'd have to push the frontier on alignment and interpretability techniques. </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to judge adequate alignment?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What process might we use to judge whether a lab has adequately resolved its alignment issues for a generation of models? Here's my vague, qualitative proposal:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Multiple independent alignment organizations examine and red-team the newest model at many checkpoints throughout training, with full access to the data and with the best tools available.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment issues need to be taken seriously whether there's a metric for it or whether something is qualitatively off, whether the model has a bad attribute or whether they can't robustly conclude it has a good one, etc.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment issues early in training may not be a showstopper, but there must be a robust safety case for why the later stages of training will resolve those issues.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A model of a new generation is ready for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">restricted deployment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> only when the alignment organizations are satisfied with it. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Restricted deployment starts with a group of trusted users doing tasks that don't contribute to model training and monitoring. The alignment organizations monitor restricted deployment for any new issues.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If any significant alignment issue arises during restricted deployment:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pull the model.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do a root cause analysis of the issue and why it was not caught during training.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure out a principled way to fix the disease rather than the symptom.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Train the next model of the current generation accordingly, and start again at the top.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A model of a new generation is ready for deployment (internal or external) only when it's survived a significant period of restricted deployment without any issues arising.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If any significant alignment issue arises during broader deployment, hit reset as outlined in the previous section.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If no alignment issues have come up over a significant stretch of deployment time, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only then</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can the lab begin training a next-generation model.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I still wouldn't say that this is fully adequate, but I'd treat it as a minimum requirement.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This proposal is incomplete; you can help by expanding it</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This isn't a concrete proposal, just a heuristic one. A few obvious missing pieces, aside from the standard implementation problems: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we define "a generation of models", within one lab or across several labs? </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should we do if an intervention to fix an alignment issue </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">also</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> significantly increases capabilities?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How would we define and enforce an agreement, if we're unable to steer solely by predefined metrics?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When is an alignment issue big enough that we should halt the model? There will be obvious pressure from the lab to set the threshold high.</span></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6n42svad2it"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6n42svad2it">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And no, "scores better than previous models on our metrics" is not adequate. If a user points out a weird but alarming thing that wasn't noticed before deployment, that is a critical failure in the process as well as in the model.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn40kow7g086n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref40kow7g086n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sure, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maybe</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your Mach 2 jet crashes for a reason that stops being a problem at Mach 3. But it would be crazy to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assume</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that!</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZWSstGSwmaxTjQ8W7/minimum-required-pacing-finish-aligning-it-before-you-train#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Not Deciphering The Voynich Manuscript](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/not-deciphering-the-voynich-manuscript)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/29774366" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Piltdown man</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was the most successful artifactual hoax of all time. The relevant skull was a chimeric melange of orangutan and human bone fragments from several individuals “discovered” in England in 1912, and meant to represent a transitional form between apes and humans. Given the intense interest in evolutionary biology, it took almost fifty years to discover the Piltdown fabrication. The study of human evolution was derailed. Adding insult to injury, a further sixty years was required to conclusively demonstrate the identity of the culpable fraudster. If you must look up the identity of this scientific blasphemer, go ahead, but we won’t give his name space here.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791721881/lexical_client_uploads/luzr6pp6t8xdmyhn0ns7.png" /><figcaption><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 1. The different parts of the Piltdown Man skull</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Students of fraud and fabrication always combat headwinds. In the winter. Uphill both ways.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The evidentiary standards for such claims of fabrication grow higher yet given the passage of centuries and an unknown creator of the subject of interest. And so we arrive at the Voynich manuscript.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s a book some 240 pages long, proved by carbon dating techniques to have originated sometime in the 15th century, in the style of the late Italian middle ages. No known author or point of origin. It contains fanciful drawings and is written in an unknown script or language. Today, the physical artifact resides at a Yale library. A small literature devoted to the study of this object has originated, its mystique derived from the uncracked code or untranslated language that make up the book.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My personal view is that this manuscript is a completely meaningless fabrication, the Piltdown man of vellum text artifacts. An elaborate, extended medieval doodle, though it’s impossible to divine the scribe’s intention exactly. This post will marshal and present some of the evidence for such an interpretation, though admittedly, it doesn’t rise to the modern evidentiary standards of fraud. Like I said, uphill, both ways.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of the information about the physical artifact itself comes from René Zandbergen’s </span><a href="https://voynich.nu/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wonderfully done website</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If you want to go down this rabbit hole, I’d start there. As I mentioned before, there’s also a scholarly literature on Voynich and it’s collected a lot of interest across the internet, for a variety of different reasons. Zandbergen even gives the following piece of helpful advice.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791721958/lexical_client_uploads/oji5lbxtll9w9x7hk9ft.png" /><figcaption><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 2. A grouchy person.</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Voynich manuscript was captured on vellum, a type of parchment made from calfskin. In the process of obtaining data and information for this post, I naturally gathered all the photographed raw page-level images. The book starts out as a collection of unknown, fanciful plants, the so-called “Herbal Section”.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791722354/lexical_client_uploads/vssjo8zhqh2eaeplliva.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 3. A made up plant in the herbal section</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point the subject matter switches to what might be described as almost gnostic.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791722012/lexical_client_uploads/aviiwqr575wwjeatihad.png" /><figcaption><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 4. A “Zodiac” page of the Voynich manuscript.</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791722030/lexical_client_uploads/mfwewsqjdfqpnwwb9ijk.png" /><figcaption><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 5. Note the doodly-repetition.</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note the visual motifs repeated on each page. Some are human or animal motifs; in the earlier pages, the plants’ leaves and appendages themselves also repeat, though it looks a little better given that plants actually do have many leaves that look quite similar. Keep an eye on the theme of repetition because we’ll come back to this idea. A central question here is does this art look more like my favorite medieval tapestry, created at about the same time as Voynich</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Unicorn Lore: Interpreting the Lady and the Unicorn Tapestries –  #FolkloreThursday" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/ajteaqemwndbfmvtshwa" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 6. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Lady and the Unicorn </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tapestry.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or does it look more like an art class doodle which was not created at that time?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="High School art lesson: Doodle Art Compositions | Davis Publications" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/ct4deotmz7wgouuznz6t" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 7. Doodling</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think you can probably guess my answer to this question. Maybe my comparison of artwork in a vellum book to a giant tapestry is unfair, but you can also look at illuminated manuscripts from the 15th century (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_hours" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">books of hours</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are good examples) to see comparators drawn on a similar medium and format, and with a real language.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/lcedf1bgmhgb6vlnq5wx" /><figcaption><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 8. A 15th century illuminated manuscript with real words on it.</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since I’m not an art historian, and don’t even play one on TV, the bulk of this post will focus on the written Voynich elements, not the illustrated ones. They’re much more amenable to statistical analysis. As you can see, the latter pages of the book tend to be full pages of text written in the same mysterious script you see above.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/c2acptuntzkpt7wadk5f" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 9. A full page of Voynich text.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are not Latin letters in fancy medieval handwriting. They don’t resemble any known written language or writing system. The Voynich text corpus is truly unique. The text features repeated but modified word motifs. Words depend on the words directly preceding them, which suggests a formulaic generative process, not a meaningful excerpt of natural language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There have been several independent transcription attempts to digitally encode the Voynich manuscript. As far as I can tell, they are all high quality and quite similar to each other which serves our purposes well here, though for this analysis, I used the Zandbergen-Landini transcription.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Statistical Analysis</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this uniqueness, the hypothesis space about what the written Voynich script means is complicated. Is it an unknown natural language, a known natural language in an unknown script, cipher-text of a known natural language, a shorthand language known to only a select few (maybe just one person), or is it </span><a href="https://www.history.com/articles/voynich-manuscript-mystery" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alien language</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> guidebook abandoned on Earth hundreds of years ago, a la the History Channel? I certainly don’t mind entertaining disreputable ideas here. Some of these hypotheses are falsifiable though, and the process here is to systematically evaluate how likely each of these ideas is based on different statistical strategies used to evaluate the Voynich text.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The original work here actually started a year or so ago. After I got started, I did consult some of the computational literature that has accreted about Voynich, and some of what I’m doing here is simply replicating the results of others from the digitally transliterated text, which looks like this in my IDE.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/htxbl5jfu5ushmzrmywn" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 10. Captain Currier’s transcribed Voynich manuscript, on my computer.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of the computational work here is my own, even though I’m not necessarily breaking new ground. So first things first. Is the Voynich manuscript comprised of a natural language?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conditional Entropy</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was the cornerstone statistic of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2010.14697" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lindemann and Bowern, 2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which compares glyph-level conditional entropy across hundreds of real languages to the supposed Voynichese language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conditional entropy measures how much unpredictability remains concerning a new glyph once you know the glyph before it. Lindemann and Bowern do a more thorough job than I do going over hundreds of different comparator languages but you can see the money shot in my figure here.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/mpda3ne38pspja5emtwc" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 11. A highly constrained language, if it’s a language.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All the real written languages here, even made up ones like Esperanto, fall within a pretty small entropy band here. The lower the conditional entropy figure is, the more previous glyphs predict about the next ones. Voynichese is a massive outlier here, and looks much more like a literal Markov chain than a real language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Remember, this is a log base 2 scale. The Voynichese 1.3 bit deficit here compared to Latin might not sound like much, but given a preceding glyph, Voynichese behaves as if choosing among roughly 4 equally-likely continuations where Latin has about 10 such choices. Our figure here reproduces Lindemann and Bowern’s Figure 11.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/eczjcixmntycbvpu4t50" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 12. Lindemann and Bowern’s excellent conditional entropy calculations across a huge number of writing systems.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These authors are Voynich believers and think the document presents an authentic, meaningful written text. On the idea of fabrication, they state</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The major unsolved question of the Voynich text is whether it represents meaningful language. It could be a medieval hoax that is designed to look like an esoteric alchemical text, in which no sequences of letters correspond to any meaningful words or concepts (see Rugg 2004; Timm and Schinner 2020, amongst others). </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If so, the creators did an incredible job of imitating the patterns of an authentic language text considering what was known about the structure of language at the time</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In so doing, they must have modeled their fake language after a real language that they were familiar with, imbuing it with familiar, language-like patterns.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know how this statement squares with Figure 12. Their paper is entitled </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character Entropy in Modern and Historical Texts: Comparison Metrics for an Undeciphered Manuscript, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and so character entropy is their central statistic whose calculation plainly contradicts the statement above. A footnote adds a bit more context and even intrigue.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Timm and Schinner 2021 make this point especially forcefully, even going as far as accusing us of a lack of scientific rigor for not finding their arguments convincing. While the aim of this paper is to make language comparisons across natural and constructed languages, rather than to make arguments about any particular theory, we reiterate a point we have made several times elsewhere (including in the review article that they criticize): that Voynichese appears unnatural only below the word level. At the level of page and paragraph, Voynichese is comparable to natural language and structured text.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their results show that Voynichese is unusual at the glyph level. The interpretative dispute is whether its regularities distinguish a meaningful language excerpt from plausible text-generation procedures that people like Timm have proposed. So I think they recognize this issue, but prefer to rely on other higher level structural statistics. How informative are those statistics?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zipf’s Law</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is now a word-level analysis, as Lindemann and Bowern prefer. Zipf’s law states that in natural languages, a word’s rank order is inversely proportional to its frequency in a corpus. So, in a linguistic corpus, a word second ranked in order of frequency is used more often than the third rank ordered word, etc. No one understands precisely why natural languages obey this law, but they do, and Zipf-like laws are a workhorse for some areas of computational linguistics and cryptography. Zipf-like properties are necessary conditions for a corpus to form part of a natural language, but as we will see, they’re not sufficient to establish that.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/cnnburpvxy8hmktuatqw" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 13. Voynich is Zipf-like.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unlike with the conditional entropy calculations, Voynichese sits squarely in the natural language band. Right along with other notable classical authors like Dante, Goethe, and an Order 1 Markov process. The point is that Zipf-ness is not strong evidence of this being a natural language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To my surprise, I don’t think anyone in the Voynich literature has explicitly reported this statistic for the manuscript. </span><a href="https://aclanthology.org/W11-1511.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reddy and Knight, 2011</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> state that it “follows Zipf’s law”, but don’t report the parameters. It’s 1.854, as reported above. Maybe everyone thinks this is so trivial that it’s not worth mentioning, but you heard it here first. I suppose this is an example of what Lindemann and his colleagues mean by “imitating the patterns of an authentic language text”.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Local Dependence</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Torsten Timm is the guy who’s done the most work studying the Voynich’s local word dependence. It’s long been known that Voynich seems to have similarly spelled words close together, and the lines and paragraphs look more systematically integrated than you might expect from a natural language. The swashbuckling linguist and cryptographer Captain Prescott H. Currier </span><a href="https://voynich.nu/extra/img/curr_main.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">first noticed this in 1976</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and on the structural Voynich issues he raises in his monograph, gives us this gem.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These [</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">structural</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">] findings are definite enough, I think, to warrant much further study by anyone who is going to be involved in seriously attacking the text of the Voynich manuscript. I have no interpretations of them, by the way; I have no solutions. All I know is that they are significant — and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">damn</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> significant. Anyone who attempts to work on the text without considering these, ignores them at his own peril.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So Currier’s not sure what to make of the book’s text, but </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1407.6639" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Timm builds upon these ideas in 2016</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and makes an appearance in the footnote quoted above. He thinks that Voynich is a hoax or fabrication.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In real books, words and close variations of those words can get repeated within paragraphs and within pages. This happens because there’s thematic correlation: pages and paragraphs close together in a book are talking about similar things, and similar things are often described using similar words, or the same words are repeated. But Timm states this isn’t what’s happening here, and provides some pretty good tests proving it. He says</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In natural languages a word normally (cf. poems) is used because of its meaning and not because it is similar to a previously written one. The result that the words are arranged such that they co-occur with similar ones is therefore not compatible with a linguistic system. An English text with similar features would mainly consist of words similar to the words “the”, “and” and “to”. Additionally, a word “the” would co-occur with words like “khe”, “phe”, “fhe”, “tha”, “tho”, “thy”, “thee” and “theee”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">His contention is that the level of word lookalikes and co-occurrence in Voynichese exceeds what can be explained by thematic correlation or by linguistic inflection if it’s actually meaningful text. My own analysis concurs. Over the pages of the manuscript, I looked how many times you see adjacent word lookalikes. In English, these would look like single edit distance pairs like “book took”, “tick tock”, or “wow how”.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/vbgrx3j6ove3ar3csqzk" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 14. Adjacent word lookalikes by page in the Voynich manuscript.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A page whose vocabulary is concentrated in a small number of words produces such pairs by chance. The green series, the same tokens reordered, is the baseline the blue must be read against. Words further apart from each other are not measured here, but we do that in the next analysis. Against a Latin baseline you see this enormous enrichment in adjacent lookalikes, and the effect persists after you control for the vocabulary on a page; Timm correctly points out how strange this is. It’s much better accounted for by a scribe semi-systematically modifying new words using past words as a template. This is supported by some of the inter-line level analysis he does.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/gyuyjxj7vc9xgs2vacki" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 15. Vertical similarity across adjacent Voynich lines, from Timm, 2016.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So you don’t just see motifs preserved but slightly changed in adjacent Voynich words. You see it vertically between lines as well. It’s as if the scribe looks around what’s recently been written, copies some kind of base template, and makes a modification. I’d remind you that this is entirely of a piece with the conditional entropy calculations above and some of the repetitive, formulaic illustrations.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/blb1bk4gkplvkkzbmx2r" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 16. Single visual motif with dancing women, small changes.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GRU</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above analysis only looks at adjacent words. I’ve been on a bit of a </span><a href="https://goflaw.substack.com/p/a-statistical-audit-of-emily-wilsons?r=41a8s8" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">machine learning applied to old texts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> kick lately, and wanted to try my hand here. There have not been many ML-themed methods applied to the Voynich problem over the past few years, at least in a scholarly setting. I found </span><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/voynich/comments/1fd6zmc/training_language_models_on_voynich/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this guy on Reddit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> who tried something similar to what I’ll do here, but with a somewhat different strategy and intent.</span></p><p><a href="https://d2l.ai/chapter_recurrent-modern/gru.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GRU is a Gated Recurrent Unit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This neural network architecture is what preceded the modern transformer architecture that make up all the modern LLMs. Like the transformer, this model is trained on text to predict the next token in a string of language. The conditional entropy statistic we examined before only knows about the token immediately preceding what you’re trying to predict. Our GRU can look at a hundred or more glyphs preceding what we’re trying to predict and model long term dependencies. GRUs were replaced by transformers because they can’t be trained at the massive, parallel scale that enables modern AI systems, but the Voynich manuscript is not a massive dataset, and what was old is now new.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I trained this neural network on a random subset of the Voynich manuscript’s pages, along with several control models on corpora of comparable size but with different underlying properties or an entirely different language. The idea is to test out the strength of the local copying effects described before: if you mask a previous Voynich lookalike word, how much worse does the trained model do predicting the downstream lookalike compared to when you do it with a real language? Functionally, this means you make a fake token like </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[mask], </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">randomly include it during training so the model is accustomed to seeing it, and then if you see “paw” followed by “caw” later in the sentence, “paw” gets replaced with the three letter fake word </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[mask][mask][mask].</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If there are these local copying effects in the manuscript, the Voynich model is going to do worse than the trained control models over many such predictive rollouts, and the effect can be quantified. We get this, the central finding of this post.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/zwohhk0nwkfnftd8hjvs" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 17. Predictive degradation in Voynichese.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So even though the Voynichese GRU model is trained to predictive quality comparable to a GRU model underlying Timm’s preferred copy-method that he </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1407.6639" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">asserts explains the generation of the Voynich manuscript</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or a Markov process, masking a lookalike word rather than a randomly chosen word degrades the model performance much more in Voynichese than it does in a Markov process or a real language like Latin. In other words, the trained model relies on the earlier lookalike in downstream prediction. Timm’s method produces the same effect to a slightly larger degree than Voynichese, but keep in mind it’s entirely synthetic and built to do that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hold on there a sec, RBA! Haven’t you heard of inflected languages? Latin is one of them. Nouns and adjectives change from a base form depending on what they’re doing in a sentence. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Puer </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is the Latin word for boy when the boy is a sentence’s subject but it’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">puerum </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">when the boy is the object. Those sure look like similar words with a small change and Latin definitely is a real language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the idea that this is morphology doesn’t hold water even under a fairly broad set of potential morphological templates.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/d7skkwpcevm19cbmfgv6" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 18. Voynich uses very unusual inflection patterns if it is inflected.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The lookalike degradation in Voynichese is highest when the lookalike word pairs differ in the glyphs at the beginning and end, which is unlike real languages like Latin or Esperanto. I was surprised to find this convoluted construction has actually been proposed as the primary inflection mechanism in Voynichese. </span><a href="https://aclanthology.org/W11-1511.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reddy and Knight, 2011</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> state</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Several hypotheses about VMS word structure have been proposed. Tiltman (1967) proposed a template consisting of roots and suffixes. Stolfi (2005) breaks down the morphology into ‘prefix-midfix-suffix’, where the letters in the midfixes are more or less disjoint from the letters in the suffixes and prefixes. Stolfi later modified this to a ‘core-mantel-crust’ model, where words are composed of three nested layers.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As with the conditional entropy statistics, I couldn’t find any evidence that any meaningful written language uses this tripartite inflection structure at anything like the Voynichese rate, but as you can see from the figure, this makes sense if Timm is right and this is a copy and edit scheme a knavish medieval prankster is deploying.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So if the Voynich manuscript’s text doesn’t look like a natural or even constructed language, what’s left?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On this point, Lindemann’s claim that a 15th century prankster could not possibly have known about Zipf’s law or other statistical requirements for a hoax cuts both ways. This could be a cipher-text of a known or unknown language. It could even be a sophisticated verbose cipher in which a single plaintext character gets carried into multiple cipher-text glyphs, but I don’t think the vocabulary math works out there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But just as no one had discovered Zipf’s law, a lot less was known about cryptography in the 15th century than is known now. This shrinks the viability of these parts of the hypothesis space quite a bit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The conditional entropy calculations for the Voynich manuscript we and other investigators have done exclude simple substitution ciphers. What’s interesting here is that the 15th century milieu from which Voynich originated was sort of a turning point for cryptographic technology. People understood that frequency analysis made substitution ciphers very vulnerable to a determined code-breaker and the innovations at the time demonstrate that. Leon Battista Alberti invented polyalphabetic ciphers in 1467. Those actually increase conditional entropy and so I’m skeptical that Alberti’s theoretical breakthrough can rescue the cryptographic Voynich hypothesis but it is notable nonetheless. Mary Queen of Scots</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">used a nomenclator cipher (also increases conditional entropy!) in 1586, over a century after Alberti, had it broken by Thomas Phelippes, and it cost her her head. The Voynich document clearly is not substitution enciphered text from the language we looked at here, or any known language for that matter. Figure 12 is striking, the most important figure in this post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are other options. What about a compressed or shorthand language meant for a small enclave of people? What about the most extreme example, a shorthand language for a single person? Wouldn’t that be unbreakable with no secondary reference text? I have some other work addressing these possibilities I might present in a part two of this post, but you’ll have to wait and see.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s also a taboo in action that’s worth mentioning here, and I don’t mind breaking it. There is this common fascination with the ancient, the mysterious, the occult, and the Voynich manuscript is the mayor of that space. The zodiac drawings, the secret language, potential hidden ancient wisdom might have a very strong hold on people, even academics at Yale. This is why people watch the History Channel and why Joe Rogan can’t quite ever shut up about how it was aliens or some ancient advanced technology that allowed the pyramids to be built. Even though there were scientists skeptical about Piltdown Man from the very beginning, people wanted Piltdown Man to fill in for missing evolutionary evidence, and so the hoax went on. The conditional entropy calculations make Voynich a massive information theoretic outlier, and even sophisticated people are willing to give it the benefit of the doubt.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coda</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s 2026. I’d like to point out that I’m making myself particularly vulnerable calling the Voynich a fabrication or meaningless piece of doodling. Just the past few weeks, the most powerful LLMs have made cryptographic breakthroughs unusually likely. During that time, </span><a href="https://simonklee.dk/farnese-letter" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simon Klee cracked a hitherto unbroken papal cipher</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to read a letter that hadn’t been read since the 16th century. Literally the day after that was published, </span><a href="https://www.prinzai.com/p/gpt-6-astra-solves-a-wwi-german-radio" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astra solved an unbroken German cipher from WWI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I promised a part two, but I might look like an idiot </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">soon.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Adxnsud5siZTRAaT3/not-deciphering-the-voynich-manuscript#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase


-->

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic engineering, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>
